{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.5145}{0.85748}{-0.85748}{0.5145}{7.2458pt}{4.8208pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{65}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,.

(45)

The identity (
44
) trivially holds true since
{\mathcal{T}}^{-}_{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau_{i})=\mathcal{R}_{{\mathfrak{e}}}^{-}(\tau_{i})=\{\bullet\}
for
i=1,2
, as the operation defining
\mathcal{R}_{{\mathfrak{e}}}^{-}(\tau_{2})
treats the edges stemming from a pair of vertices sharing the same exotic decoration as a single one. By the inductive hypothesis we can write

\displaystyle\frac{|\tau|!}{\tau!}=\sum_{\tau^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}^{-}_{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau)}\frac{|\tau^{\prime}|!}{\tau^{\prime}!}=\sum_{\tau^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}^{-}_{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau)}\sum_{\tau^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{{\mathfrak{e}}}^{-}(\tau^{\prime})}\frac{|\tau^{\prime\prime}|!}{\tau^{\prime\prime}!}\,.

We observe that the two operations that define
{\mathcal{T}}^{-}_{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau)
and
\mathcal{R}_{{\mathfrak{e}}}^{-}(\tau)
commute. Indeed, if
\tau^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}^{-}_{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau)
was generated by subtracting a leaf attached to the root, then
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{{\mathfrak{e}}}^{-}(\tau)
as well. If this was not the case, then the sets of edges on which the two operations act do not intersect. Here asking that pairs of
\beta
-vertices that are not both linked to the root do not form effective edges is crucial.
As a result

\displaystyle\frac{|\tau|!}{\tau!}=\sum_{\tau^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{{\mathfrak{e}}}^{-}(\tau)}\sum_{\tau^{\prime\prime}\in{\mathcal{T}}^{-}_{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau^{\prime})}\frac{|\tau^{\prime\prime}|!}{\tau^{\prime\prime}!}=\sum_{\tau^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{{\mathfrak{e}}}^{-}(\tau)}\frac{|\tau^{\prime}|!}{\tau^{\prime}!}\,.

This concludes the proof.

This alternative characterization of the tree factorial for trees lying in
{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\alpha,\beta}
is of paramount importance to express the realization map as a power of the time parameter. Indeed, a direct inspection of Definition
2.6
suggests that the operation defining
\mathcal{R}_{{\mathfrak{e}}}^{-}(\tau)
realizes the action of time derivatives on
\Pi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{t}(\cdot)
. This observation is at the heart of the following statement.

Proposition 2.27

Let
\Pi^{\mathfrak{e}}:{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\mathfrak{e}}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
be the realization map of Definition
2.6
. Then, for any
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\mathfrak{e}}
and
t\in[0,T]
we have

\Pi_{t}^{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau)=\frac{|\tau|!}{\tau!}\frac{t^{\mathfrak{l}(\tau)}}{\mathfrak{l}(\tau)!}\,.

(46)

Proof.

We shall consider the action of the time derivative on the realization map. By definition, see (
22
), the only time dependence is carried by the kernels representing edges that connect the root to the rest of the tree. Since the weak derivative of the Heaviside Theta is a Dirac delta, this operation boils down to identifying the vertices at the extrema of the edge hit by the derivative. Observe that, if the time derivative falls on a non-effective edge, this identification forms a closed path. Then, the oriented nature of
\Theta
forces such contribution to vanish. Hence, at a graphical level, taking the derivative of the realization map coincides with the operation that defines the set
\mathcal{R}_{{\mathfrak{e}}}^{-}
. Hence

\frac{d}{dt}\Pi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{t}(\tau)=\sum_{\tau^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{{\mathfrak{e}}}^{-}(\tau)}\Pi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{t}(\tau^{\prime})\,,

(47)

At the same time, by deriving the right-hand side of (
2.27
) we have

\frac{d}{dt}{\left(\frac{|\tau|!}{\tau!}\frac{t^{\mathfrak{l}(\tau)}}{\mathfrak{l}(\tau)!}\right)}=\frac{|\tau|!}{\tau!}\frac{t^{\mathfrak{l}(\tau)-1}}{(\mathfrak{l}(\tau)-1)!}

(48)

We will use this information in the induction on the number of vertices to prove (
46
). A direct inspection show that the latter trivially holds true for
\tau_{1}
and
\tau_{2}
as in (
45
). Now assume that (
46
) is satisfied by any tree with at most
n
vertices and we consider
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\alpha,\beta}
such that
|\tau|=n+1
.
From the equivalence shown in Theorem
2.26
and by exploiting the induction hypothesis on
\tau^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{{\mathfrak{e}}}^{-}(\tau)
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{{\mathfrak{e}}}^{-}(\tau)}\Pi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{t}(\tau^{\prime})=\sum_{\tau^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{{\mathfrak{e}}}^{-}(\tau)}\frac{|\tau^{\prime}|!}{\tau^{\prime}!}\frac{t^{\mathfrak{l}(\tau^{\prime})}}{\mathfrak{l}(\tau^{\prime})!}=\Bigl(\sum_{\tau^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{{\mathfrak{e}}}^{-}(\tau)}\frac{|\tau^{\prime}|!}{\tau^{\prime}!}\Bigr)\frac{t^{\mathfrak{l}(\tau)-1}}{(\mathfrak{l}(\tau)-1)!}=\frac{|\tau|!}{\tau!}\frac{t^{\mathfrak{l}(\tau)-1}}{(\mathfrak{l}(\tau)-1)!}\,.

As a result

\frac{d}{dt}{\left(\Pi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{t}(\tau)-\frac{|\tau|!}{\tau!}\frac{t^{\mathfrak{l}(\tau)}}{\mathfrak{l}(\tau)!}\right)}=0\,.

Since the solution to this differential equation must have vanishing initial condition at
t=0
, we conclude that

\Pi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{t}(\tau)=\frac{|\tau|!}{\tau!}\frac{t^{\mathfrak{l}(\tau)}}{\mathfrak{l}(\tau)!}\,.

Remark 2.28

Proving (
46
) without passing through the equivalent definition of tree factorial of Theorem
2.26
is a daunting task. A major difficulty resides in the process of constructing trees lying in
{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\mathfrak{e}}
, which does not boil down to products of trees and convolutions with a kernel, at the heart of the algebraic integration adopted in
e.g.
rough paths and regularity structures. To wit, the nonlocal features of exotic decorations produce trees excluded from the image of such simple operations. Another point of view, presented in Appendix
A
, involves a reduction map that sends exotic coloured trees to undecorated rooted trees via an algorithm inspired by the analytic information encoded within closed paths. Even if this strategy does not lead to a result analogous to Theorem
2.26
, a comparison unveils intriguing combinatorial features of exotic trees in connection with standard rooted trees.

Corollary 2.29

Let
\alpha,\beta,f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be real analytic functions such that (
1
) admits a unique solution
u
with initial condition
u_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
on
[0,T]
. Then

\mathbb{E}^{u_{0}}[f(u_{t})]=\sum_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\alpha,\beta}}\frac{\Upsilon_{u_{0}}^{\alpha,\beta,f}(\tau)}{\sigma_{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau)}\Pi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{t}(\tau)\,,

(49)

as a formal series.

Proof.

The statement follows from combining the formal exotic B-series expansion in (
43
) and Proposition
2.27
.

3
MSR expectations via Feynman multi-indices

As underlined in the introduction, the perturbative expansion of MSR expectations heavily relies on the interpretation of the exponential of the free action as a Gaussian measure on a space of paths
(\psi,\tilde{\psi})
. Indeed, let us consider the Hilbert space
\mathcal{C}\oplus\tilde{\mathcal{C}}=H^{1}([0,T])\oplus H^{1}([0,T])
and the operator
T:H^{1}([0,T])\oplus H^{1}([0,T])\to L^{2}([0,T])\oplus L^{2}([0,T])
defined as

T:=\begin{pmatrix}0&d_{t}^{\ast}\\
d_{t}&0\end{pmatrix}

where
d_{t}^{\ast}
denotes the adjoint of the first derivative
d_{t}
. A direct computation shows that, denoting by
\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rangle
the scalar product in
L^{2}([0,T])\oplus L^{2}([0,T])
,

\int_{0}^{T}\tilde{\psi}_{t}\dot{\psi}_{t}\,dt=\langle(\psi,\tilde{\psi}),T(\psi,\tilde{\psi})\rangle\,,

i.e.
, the free action in (
4
) is a bilinear form induced by
T
, whose inverse reads

T^{-1}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&\Theta\\
\Theta^{\ast}&0\end{pmatrix}\,

(50)

for
\Theta:L^{2}([0,T])\to L^{2}([0,T])
the operator of convolution with the Heaviside theta and
\Theta^{\ast}
its adjoint. Then
T^{-1}
is usually interpreted as the covariance matrix of the Gaussian measure
(\psi,\tilde{\psi})
, whose non-diagonal structure suggests the properties (
6
). Yet, even if
T^{-1}
is symmetric on the subset of
H^{1}([0,T])\oplus H^{1}([0,T])
of paths
\tilde{\psi}
such that
\tilde{\psi}(0)=\tilde{\psi}(T)=0
, the requirement of being non-negative cannot be satisfied, preventing us from interpreting
e^{-\langle(\psi,\tilde{\psi}),T(\psi,\tilde{\psi})\rangle}\mathcal{D}\psi\mathcal{D}\tilde{\psi}
as a Gaussian measure. Indeed , if
\int_{0}^{T}\tilde{\psi}_{t}\dot{\psi}_{t}\,dt>0
for a pair
(\psi,\tilde{\psi})\in\mathcal{C}\oplus\tilde{\mathcal{C}}
, then
\int_{0}^{T}\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}_{t}\dot{\psi}^{\prime}_{t}\,dt<0
for
(\psi^{\prime},\tilde{\psi}^{\prime})=(\psi,-\tilde{\psi})\in\mathcal{C}\oplus\tilde{\mathcal{C}}
.
This remark invalidates the usual, heuristic derivation of path integral expectations in the MSR formalism. Nevertheless, explicit computations in manifold application of this framework have proven efficient in reproducing the correct statistics of the diffusion process under scrutiny. Hence we are led to believe that this improper assumption leads to the right results. Our strategy is thus to give up on the probabilistic viewpoint on the construction of Feynman diagrams and to define suitable operations that mimic the behaviour of Gaussian fields. A posteriori we will show that this assumption on the construction of the MSR expectations yields the correct result.

We shall adopt the functional point of view on perturbative algebraic quantum field theory
[
BDF+15
,
HR20
]
, in which additional information from the model are implemented at the algebraic level via a deformation of the product structure of suitable spaces of functionals over smooth configurations.
As advocated in
[
BDD25
]
, the problem of constructing MSR expectations via maximal contractions of the auxiliary fields can be rigorously tackled by resorting to a formulation based on distribution-valued functionals. Since our ultimate goal is just to rigorously define a map that implements at the analytic level the contraction of vertices to form Feynman diagrams, we present directly the form of the MSR expectations in term of distribution-valued functionals and contraction maps. For the sake of readability we collect all definitions in Appendix
C
, as they are not crucial to understand the rest of the paper.

The MSR action (
1
) can be represented as a distribution-valued functional lying in
\mathcal{F}_{loc}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})
:

S_{int}[\psi,\psi]:\quad\mapsto\quad\mathsf{S}(\psi,\tilde{\psi}):=\tilde{\Psi}\alpha(\Psi)-\theta_{0}\alpha^{(1)}(\Psi)+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\Psi}^{2}\beta^{2}(\Psi)-\theta_{0}\tilde{\Psi}\beta(\Psi)\beta^{(1)}(\Psi)\,,

(51)

where
\Psi,\tilde{\Psi}
are the field functionals defined in (
78
). In particular, defining
\chi(t):=\Theta(t)-\Theta(t-T)
,

S_{int}[\psi,\psi]:=\langle\mathsf{S}(\psi,\tilde{\psi}),\chi\rangle\,.

Notice that, even if
\chi
is not a test function lying in
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R})
, the pairing in (
51
) is well defined. Then the MSR expectation of a function
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
of the diffusion process solving (
1
) is defined as the formal perturbative series

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965588}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965587}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle MSR$}}}}}}[f(u_{t})]:=\Gamma_{\Theta}{\left[f(\Psi)\cdot e^{\mathsf{S}_{int}(\Psi,\Psi)}\right]}(\delta_{t}\otimes\chi)\Big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\psi=u_{0}\\
\tilde{\psi}=0\end{subarray}}

The action of
\Gamma_{\Theta}
amounts to contracting in all possible ways an increasing number of pairs
(\Psi,\tilde{\Psi})
into an instance of the integral kernel of the Heaviside theta.
For notational ease we shall write distribution-valued functionals in terms of their integral kernels. Accordingly, the latter expression can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965588}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965587}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle MSR$}}}}}}[f(u_{t})]

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\Theta}{\left[f(\psi(t))\cdot e^{\langle\tilde{\psi}\alpha(\psi)-\theta_{0}\alpha^{(1)}(\psi)+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\psi}^{2}\beta^{2}(\psi)-\theta_{0}\tilde{\psi}\beta(\psi)\beta^{(1)}(\psi),\chi\rangle}\right]}\Big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\psi=u_{0}\\
\tilde{\psi}=0\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\Theta}{\left[f(\psi(t))\cdot e^{\langle\Gamma_{\Theta}^{-1}(\widetilde{\psi}\alpha(\psi))+\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\psi}^{2}\beta^{2}(\psi)-\theta_{0}\widetilde{\psi}\beta\beta^{(1)}(\psi),\chi\rangle}\right]}\Big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\psi=u_{0}\\
\tilde{\psi}=0\end{subarray}}\,

(52)

where we used the identity
\Gamma_{\Theta}^{-1}(\tilde{\psi}\alpha(\psi))=\Gamma_{-\Theta}(\tilde{\psi}\alpha(\psi))=\tilde{\psi}\alpha(\psi)-\Theta(0)\alpha^{(1)}(\psi)
and the identification
\Theta(0)=\theta_{0}
. Resorting to the nonlocal version of the contraction map

\Gamma_{\Theta}^{mloc}:\mathcal{F}_{mloc}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})\,\hat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{F}_{mloc}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{mloc}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})\,,

defined in (
82
), Equation (
52
) takes the form

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965588}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965587}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle MSR$}}}}}}[f(u_{t})]

\displaystyle=\Gamma^{mloc}_{\Theta}{\left[\Gamma_{\Theta}(f(\psi(t)))\,\hat{\otimes}\,e_{\hat{\otimes}}^{\langle\Gamma_{\Theta}\Gamma_{\Theta}^{-1}(\widetilde{\psi}\alpha(\psi))+\frac{1}{2}\Gamma_{\Theta}\widetilde{\psi}^{2}\beta^{2}(\psi)-\theta_{0}\Gamma_{\Theta}\widetilde{\psi}\beta\beta^{(1)}(\psi),\chi\rangle}\right]}\Big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\psi=u_{0}\\
\tilde{\psi}=0\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle=\Gamma^{mloc}_{\Theta}{\left[f(\psi(t))\,\hat{\otimes}\,e_{\hat{\otimes}}^{\langle\widetilde{\psi}\alpha(\psi)+\frac{1}{2}\Gamma_{\Theta}\widetilde{\psi}^{2}\beta^{2}(\psi)-\theta_{0}\Gamma_{\Theta}\widetilde{\psi}\beta\beta^{(1)}(\psi),\chi\rangle}\right]}\Big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\psi=u_{0}\\
\tilde{\psi}=0\end{subarray}}\,,

where
e_{\hat{\otimes}}
denotes the exponential of functional-valued distributions with respect to the tensor product
\hat{\otimes}
, see Appendix
C
.
On account of the identification of
\Theta(0)
with
\theta_{0}
, an explicit calculation shows that

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\Theta}{\left(\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\psi}^{2}\beta^{2}(\psi)-\theta_{0}\tilde{\psi}\beta\beta^{(1)}(\psi)\right)}(t)=\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\psi}^{2}\beta^{2}(\psi)(t)+\theta_{0}\tilde{\psi}\beta\beta^{(1)}(\psi)(t)\,.

Hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965588}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965587}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle MSR$}}}}}}[f(u_{t})]

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\Theta}^{mloc}{\left[f(\psi(t))\,\hat{\otimes}\,e_{\hat{\otimes}}^{\langle\widetilde{\psi}\alpha(\psi)+\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\psi}^{2}\beta^{2}(\psi)+\theta_{0}\widetilde{\psi}\beta\beta^{(1)}(\psi),\chi\rangle}\right]}\Big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\psi=u_{0}\\
\tilde{\psi}=0\end{subarray}}\,.

(53)

Most notably, this expression involves uniquely the multilocal contraction map. This means that no contractions between pairs of fields
(\psi,\tilde{\psi})
belonging to the same interacting vertex
\tilde{\psi}\alpha(\psi)
or
\tilde{\psi}^{2}\beta^{2}(\psi)
will appear in the calculations, preventing loops from appearing in a diagrammatic representation of the perturbative expansion. Yet there is a residue of the arbitrariness in extending
\Theta
of the form
\theta_{0}\beta\beta^{(1)}
. Since we are interested in studying the expansion of expectation for a solution of (
1
) in the sense of Itô calculus, we shall set
\theta_{0}=0
. Hence we are left with the expression

\mathbb{E}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965588}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965587}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle MSR$}}}}}}[f(u_{t})]=\Gamma_{\Theta}^{mloc}{\left[f(\psi(t))\,\hat{\otimes}\,e_{\hat{\otimes}}^{\langle\widetilde{\psi}\alpha(\psi)+\frac{1}{2}\widetilde{\psi}^{2}\beta^{2}(\psi),\chi\rangle}\right]}\Big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\psi=u_{0}\\
\tilde{\psi}=0\end{subarray}}\,.

(54)

3.1
MSR Feynman diagrams

The idea at the heart of the perturbative expansion of path integral expectations as per (
54
) is to Taylor expand the interaction around the initial condition
u_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
in powers of
\tilde{\psi}
and
\psi-u_{0}
and to contract nonlocal pairs of fields in all possible ways. A key difference with respect to usual applications in Euclidean field theory is that, in the present framework, the Lagrangian density is not a combination of polynomials of the fields. Indeed, for the sake of generality we consider the case of an SDE with smooth coefficients
\alpha,\beta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
so that, loosely speaking, the MSR action (
1
) involves an infinite number of Taylor polynomials coming from the formal expansion of
\alpha
and
\beta^{2}
.

The contraction operation codified by
\Gamma_{\Theta}
has a natural graphical representation: following the one briefly explained in the introduction, we can think of
\psi
and
\tilde{\psi}
as free legs of different nature. Hence a contraction amounts to linking the free legs representing
\psi
and
\tilde{\psi}
, respectively into an edge. This observation prompts the definition of
MSR Feynman diagrams
as connected, oriented graphs
\Gamma=(\mathcal{E}(\Gamma),\mathcal{V}(\Gamma))
identified with a set of oriented edges
\mathcal{E}(\Gamma)
and of vertices
\mathcal{V}(\Gamma)
that abide by the following rules

1.

There is a single vertex with no outgoing edges, that we call the root.

2.

Each vertex different from the root can have either one or two outgoing edges and no restriction on the number of incoming edges.

3.

There are no oriented closed paths,
i.e.
, connected collection of edges that, when followed along their orientation, start and end at the same vertex.

We denote by
\mathcal{F}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965588}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965587}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle MSR$}}}}}}
the space of MSR Feynman diagrams. Examples of diagrams abiding by these rules are

\varrho

\varrho

\varrho

while Item
3
rules out diagrams of the form

\varrho

\varrho

According to the Feynman rules, each MSR Feynman diagram represents an integral involving the kernel of the propagator
i.e.
, the Green’s function of the differential operator ruling the linear dynamics of the field theory. In this case the latter is the Heaviside step function and the integral associated to the diagram reads

\Pi_{t}(\Gamma)=\int_{[0,T]^{|\mathcal{V}|-1}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in\mathcal{E}(\Gamma)\\
e_{+}=\varrho\end{subarray}}\Theta(t-s_{e-})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}e\in\mathcal{E}(\Gamma)\\
e_{+}\neq\varrho\end{subarray}}\Theta(s_{e_{+}}-s_{e_{-}})\,ds\,,\hskip 56.9055pt\Gamma\in\mathcal{F}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965588}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965587}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle MSR$}}}}}}\,,

where
e=(e_{+},e_{-})
and the edge points towards
e_{+}
,
\varrho
is the root of
\Gamma
and
|\mathcal{V}|
denotes the cardinality of the set of vertices. Integration is over all variables associated to internal vertices. Given the compact nature of the domain of integration, no test function at the root is needed, as it would be the case in general, see
e.g

[
Hai16
, §2]
.

The motivation behind our choice of a rather specific class of Feynman diagram is dictated by the procedure outlined in the introduction for building all Feynman diagrams that contribute to the perturbative expansion of (
7
). Indeed,
\mathbb{E}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965588}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965587}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle MSR$}}}}}}[f(u_{t})]
is a formal series whose coefficients are the contributions coming from contractions of monomials of the form
\tilde{\psi}\psi^{n}
and
\tilde{\psi^{2}}\psi^{k_{1}+k_{2}}
with
f(\psi)
via the map
\Gamma_{\Theta}
, see (
54
). Observe that integration is over the interval
[0,T]
due to the specific form of the cut-off function
\chi
.

3.2
Feynman multi-indices

Equation (
54
) formalized the idea that MSR expectations are obtained by maximally contracting pairs of auxiliary fields
(\psi,\tilde{\psi})
generated by the perturbative expansion of the MSR measure around the free theory. Then, the decomposition in terms of MSR Feynman diagrams presented in the previous section follows. In this work we adopt the point of view of
[
BH25
]
on the construction and BPHZ renormalization of the cumulants generating functional for a scalar quantum field theory based on multi-indices. This change of paradigm amounts to switching from Feynman diagrams to pre-Feynman diagrams. The latter are intended as collections of vertices with free legs that can form proper Feynman diagrams after a pairing procedure analogous to the one presented above. For a polynomial interaction, one would thing of vertices as instances of the monomials characterizing the Lagrangian density, while free legs are the fields that form the monomials. The more elementary nature of pre-Feynman diagrams is grasped by multi-indices, which simply account for the frequency of vertices with a given number of free legs, ignoring the actual topology of the diagram. In the present section we show how multi-indices are well-suited for deriving an explicit expansion of the MSR expectations (
54
).

The problem under scrutiny is characterized by an action involving two general nonlinearities of the form
\tilde{\psi}\alpha(\psi)
and
\frac{1}{2}\tilde{\psi}^{2}\beta^{2}(\psi)
for
\alpha,\beta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. This is rather peculiar in comparison to standard scalar (or complex) field theories since the term involving the square of the diffusivity complicates the combinatorial aspects of the problem. To wit, aiming at a comprehensive treatment of all nonlinearities at once, care must be taken in adapting standard techniques for studying path integrals in terms of Feynman diagrams.
The stark difference between the types of nonlinearities suggests to introduce coloured multi-indices, as
e.g.
in
[
CF24
]
.

Definition 3.1

A
Feynman multi-index
is a map

\displaystyle\gamma:

\displaystyle\,\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{2}_{\leq}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\times\{0,1\}

\displaystyle(n,\mathbf{k})\mapsto\gamma(n,\mathbf{k}):=(\gamma_{\alpha}(n),\gamma_{\beta}(\mathbf{k}),\gamma_{\bullet}(n))\,,

with compact support, that is
\gamma(n,\mathbf{k})=0
for all but finitely many
(n,\mathbf{k})\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{2}
, and such that
\gamma_{\bullet}(n)=\hbox{{\indic 1}${}_{(n=m)}$}
for a fixed
m\in\mathbb{N}
. We denote by
\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}
the linear space of Feynman multi-indices endowed with the natural notion of sum

(\gamma+\gamma^{\prime})(n,\mathbf{k}):=\gamma(n,\mathbf{k})+\gamma^{\prime}(n,\mathbf{k})\,,\qquad\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}},n\in\mathbb{N},\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}_{\leq}\,.

Given abstract variables
\{z_{\alpha,n},z_{\bullet,n},z_{\beta,\mathbf{k}}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N},\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}
, a multi-index uniquely identifies a monomial of the form

z^{\gamma}:=\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N},\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}}z_{\alpha,n}^{\gamma_{\alpha}(n)}z_{\beta,\mathbf{k}}^{\gamma_{\beta}(\mathbf{k})}z_{\bullet,n}^{\gamma_{\bullet}(n)}\,.

With a slight abuse of notation, throughout the paper we will identify monomials with multi-indices and alternate between the two notations to facilitate the reader. The space of abstract monomials forms a commutative algebra when endowed with the product

z^{\gamma}z^{\gamma^{\prime}}=z^{\gamma+\gamma^{\prime}}\,\qquad\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}\,.

(55)

For later convenience, we assign to multi-indices the following gradings:

\displaystyle[\gamma]=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}(\gamma_{\alpha}(n)+\gamma_{\bullet}(n))+\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}_{\leq}}\gamma_{\beta}(\mathbf{k})\,,

(56a)

\displaystyle|\gamma|:=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}n(\gamma_{\alpha}(n)+\gamma_{\bullet}(n))+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}^{2}_{\leq}}(k_{1}+k_{2})\gamma_{\beta}(\mathbf{k})\,,

(56b)

\displaystyle\llbracket\gamma\rrbracket:=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\gamma_{\alpha}(n)+\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}_{\leq}}2\gamma_{\beta}(\mathbf{k})\,,

(56c)

as well as their restriction to a single component of the multi-index:

\displaystyle[\gamma]_{\eta}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\gamma_{\eta}(n)\,\qquad[\gamma]_{\beta}=\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}_{\leq}}\gamma_{\beta}(\mathbf{k})\,,\qquad\eta\in\{\alpha,\bullet\}\,.

(57)

These gradings have an interpretation in relation to the graphical representation of Feynman diagrams. Recalling that multi-indices codify the number of vertices with a given fertility, we regard
[\gamma]
as the length of the multi-index, which counts the total number of vertices in the associated pre-Feynman diagram. The integer
|\gamma|
accounts for the total number of free legs associated to the field
\psi
, while
\llbracket\gamma\rrbracket
counts the total number of legs representing
\tilde{\psi}
.

Given the interpretation of the multi-index components as representing the number of vertices of a given type having fixed number of outgoing legs, one would be tempted to righteously consider
\beta^{2}(u)
as the square of
\beta
and then to resort to a single-component multi-index as for
\alpha
. Yet, our choice of treating
\beta^{2}
as a different object is crucial and motivated by the problem of performing contractions, where the
local nature
of
\beta^{2}
plays a role. The symmetry of the square justifies our choice of restricting to triangular
\gamma_{\beta}
:

\gamma_{\beta}(k_{1},k_{2})\neq 0\quad\Leftrightarrow\quad k_{1}\leq k_{2}\,.

(58)

This choice will be clarified when Taylor expanding the interaction term involving
\beta^{2}
in the proof of Theorem
3.8
.

Recalling that the expansion of the MSR expectation in (
54
) boils down to assembling in all possible ways the vertices representing
\alpha
and
\beta^{2}
into Feynman diagrams whose edges are placeholders for the inverse of the time derivative, not all Feynman multi-indices lying in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}
, see Definition
3.1
, are needed. Indeed,
\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}
encompasses multi-indices which cannot give rise to Feynman diagrams after a leg contraction procedure on the vertices that they represent. To rule them out, we resort once more to the graphical interpretation of Feynman multi-indices, which yields a crucial constraint: The evaluation of the expression in (
54
) at the field configurations
\psi=u_{0},\tilde{\psi}=0
together with the recentering at
u_{0}
due to the Taylor expansion of the coefficients, prevents free legs from surviving after the contraction procedure that generate Feynman diagrams. This observation, together with the interpretation of the gradings defined above, leads to the following natural sub-class of Feynman multi-indices.

Definition 3.2

A multi-index
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}
is said to be
populated
if

\llbracket\gamma\rrbracket=|\gamma|\,.

We denote by
\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}^{p}\subset\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}
the sub-space of populated Feynman multi-indices.

Before addressing the derivation of a perturbative expansion of the MSR path integral based on multi-indices, some additional tools to account for the information carried by multi-indices must be introduced. First, the repeated action of the differential operator
D_{\Theta}
defining the contraction map naturally produces derivatives of the drift and diffusion, as well as of the test-function
f
, evaluated at the initial condition
u_{0}
. In much the same spirit of Section
2
, each multi-index
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}
uniquely identifies an
elementary differential
.

Definition 3.3

Let
\alpha,\beta,f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
u_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. The elementary differential
\Upsilon_{u_{0}}^{\alpha,\beta,f}:\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
acts on
z^{\gamma}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}
as

\Upsilon_{u_{0}}^{\alpha,\beta,f}(z^{\gamma}):=\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}(\alpha^{(n)}(u_{0}))^{\gamma_{\alpha}(n)}(f^{(n)}(u_{0}))^{\gamma_{\bullet}(n)}\prod_{\mathbf{k}=(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}_{\leq}}(\beta^{(k_{1})}(u_{0})\beta^{(k_{2})}(u_{0}))^{\gamma_{\beta}(\mathbf{k})}\,.

(59)

Observe that the expression in (
59
) is well-defined as it involves a finite number of contributions thanks to the finite support assumption on multi-indices lying in
\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}
.

Another key ingredient is the notion of symmetry factor of the objects used to label the contributions to the expansion. A reduced notion of symmetry factor for multi-indices was introduced in
[
BEFH25
]
. In this setting, we adapt the one proposed in
[
BH25
]
.

Definition 3.4

Let
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}
. We denote by
\sigma_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}(z^{\gamma})
its symmetry factor, defined as

\sigma_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}(z^{\gamma}):=\prod_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\gamma_{\alpha}(n)!(n!)^{\gamma_{\alpha}(n)+\gamma_{\bullet}(n)}\prod_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}_{\leq}}\gamma_{\beta}(\mathbf{k})!(\mathbf{k}!)^{\gamma_{\beta}(\mathbf{k})}2^{\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\gamma_{\beta}(i,i)}\,.

(60)

The expression of
\sigma_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}(z^{\gamma})
in Definition
3.4
is motivated as follows:

•

For any
n\in\mathbb{N},\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}_{\leq}
, the terms
\gamma_{\alpha}(n)!
and
\gamma_{\beta}(\mathbf{k})!
coincide with the number of permutation of vertices of the same type and same fertility, which are considered indistinguishable within the pre-Feynman diagram. Indeed, any of these permutations leaves the induced Feynman diagrams unchanged.

•

The terms
(n!)^{\gamma_{\alpha}(n)+\gamma_{\bullet}(n)}
and
(\mathbf{k}!)^{\gamma_{\beta}(\mathbf{k})}
count the number of permutations of
\psi
-legs attached to each vertex of the pre-Feynman diagram.

•

the factor
2^{\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\gamma_{\beta}(i,i)}
is related to the exchange of
\tilde{\psi}
-legs. We should think of each vertex of type
\beta
as being bipartite, each part corresponding to one of the
\beta
forming
\beta^{2}
. Accordingly, we split
\tilde{\psi}^{2}
by attaching a single auxiliary field to each part of the bipartite vertex. As a result, when the number of
\psi
-legs attached to the two parts coincide, an exchange of the
\tilde{\psi}
-legs leaves the pre-Feynman diagram unchanged. This crucial hidden symmetry of the pre-Feynman diagrams is an artefact of our choice to treat pairs of
\beta
-vertices as single ones.

We are now in a position to introduce the map which, to any pre-Feynman diagram
z^{\gamma}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}
, associates the combination of integrals corresponding to all possible Feynman diagrams that it induces.

Definition 3.5

The
realization map

\Pi:\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}\times[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by

\Pi_{t}(z_{\bullet,n})=\Pi_{t}(z_{\alpha,n})=\Pi_{t}(z_{\beta,\mathbf{k}})=0\hskip 42.67912pt\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}_{\leq}\,,

while, for
z^{\gamma}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}
such that
[\gamma]>1
,

\displaystyle\Pi_{t}(z^{\gamma}):=

\displaystyle\quad\langle\Gamma_{\Theta}^{mloc}\bigl[\prod_{m=0}^{\infty}\Psi^{k\gamma_{\bullet}(k)}\hat{\bigotimes}_{n=0}^{\infty}(\tilde{\Psi}\Psi^{n})^{\otimes\gamma_{\alpha}(n)}\hat{\bigotimes}_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{2}_{\leq}}(\tilde{\Psi}^{2}\Psi^{|\mathbf{k}|})^{\hat{\otimes}\gamma_{\beta}(\mathbf{k})}\bigr]\Big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\psi=0\\
\tilde{\psi}=0\end{subarray}},\delta_{t}\otimes\chi^{\otimes([\gamma]_{\alpha}+[\gamma]_{\beta})}\rangle\,.

(61)

Remark 3.6

In
[
BH25
]
the realization map on multi-indices differs from the one in (
3.5
) by the subtraction of all non-connected Feynman diagrams,
i.e.
products of Feynman diagrams generated by the contractions of subgroups of the vertices forming the pre-Feynman diagrams. We could in principle modify (
3.5
) by subtracting them as well. Yet, due to the oriented nature of the propagator any disconnected Feynman diagram vanishes identically, see
[
BDD25
]
.

Remark 3.7

The evaluation at
\psi=\tilde{\psi}=0
in (
3.5
) ensures that, as soon a the number of
\psi
-legs and
\tilde{\psi}
-legs in
z^{\gamma}
differs,
\Pi_{t}(z^{\gamma})=0
. As already remarked in
[
BH25
]
, this guarantees that
\Pi_{t}(z^{\gamma})=0
for all
z^{\gamma}\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}^{p}
. Hence, every time the realization map is involved, we are authorized to drop the population condition. We stress that in (
54
) distributional-valued functionals are evaluated at
\psi=u_{0}
. In the proof of Theorem
3.8
we will show that, by expanding the all functions of the field functional
\Psi
around
u_{0}\mathbf{1}
, such evaluation coincides with centering fields on the vanishing configuration.

3.3
Derivation of Equation
9

In this section we derive an expansion of the path integral expectations of the solution to (
1
) labelled by Feynman multi-indices. Our strategy is reminiscent of the one adopted in the proof of
[
BH25
, Lem. 4.12]
to derive a formal expansion of the cumulants generating functional for a generic scalar field theory.

Theorem 3.8

Let
\alpha,\beta,f
be real analytic functions and fix
u_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. In addition, let
\Upsilon_{u_{0}}^{\alpha,\beta}
and
\Pi
be the maps introduced in Definition
3.3
and
3.5
, respectively.
Then, denoting by
u
the solution of (
1
) with initial value
u_{0}
, the path integral expectation value
\mathbb{E}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965588}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965587}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle MSR$}}}}}}[f(u_{t})]
,
t\in[0,T]
, is represented by the formal series

\mathbb{E}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965588}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965587}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle MSR$}}}}}}[f(u_{t})]=\sum_{\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{399347}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{399347}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299972}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}}\frac{\Upsilon_{u_{0}}^{\alpha,\beta,f}(z^{\gamma})}{\sigma_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}(z^{\gamma})}\Pi_{t}(z^{\gamma})\,,

(62)

where
\sigma_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}(z^{\gamma})
is the symmetry factor defined in (
60
).

Proof.

As a preliminary step we identify the interacting potentials that characterize the MSR action with their Taylor series around
u_{0}\mathbf{1}
. Let us denote by
r_{\alpha},r_{\beta},r_{f}>0
the radii of convergence of
\alpha
,
\beta
and
f
around
u_{0}
, respectively and let us set
r:=\mathop{\mathrm{min}}\{r_{\alpha},r_{\beta},r_{f}\}
. Given the auxiliary nature of the smooth configurations
\psi,\tilde{\psi}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, we have the right to cast upon them additional constraints. In particular, we shall pick
\psi
such that
|\psi(t)-u_{0}|<r
for
t\in[0,T]
. Under this assumption, the Taylor expansions of
\alpha(\psi(t))
and
\beta(\psi(t))
around
u_{0}
are absolutely convergent. Hence we are in the position to consider

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}(t)\alpha(\psi(t))=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha^{(\ell)}(u_{0})}{\ell!}\tilde{\psi}(t)(\psi(t)-u_{0})^{\ell}\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}(t)^{2}\beta^{2}(\psi(t))=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}(\beta^{2})^{(n)}(u_{0})\tilde{\psi}(t)^{2}(\psi(t)-u_{0})^{n}

\displaystyle\quad=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n!}\Bigl(\sum_{k=0}^{n}\binom{n}{k}\beta^{(k)}(u_{0})\beta^{(n-k)}(u_{0})\Bigr)\tilde{\psi}(t)^{2}(\psi(t)-u_{0})^{n}

\displaystyle=2\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n<k\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{n!k!}\beta^{(n)}(u_{0})\beta^{(k)}(u_{0})\tilde{\psi}(t)^{2}(\psi(t)-u_{0})^{n+k}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(n!)^{2}}(\beta^{(n)}(u_{0}))^{2}\tilde{\psi}(t)^{2}(\psi(t)-u_{0})^{2n}\,.

The separation in the last line is crucial, as it will become apparent in the following. The same procedure must be carried over for
f(\psi(t))
:

f(\psi(t))=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{f^{(k)}(u_{0})}{k!}(\psi(t)-u_{0})^{k}\,.

As a second step we address the expansion of the exponential of the action (
1
) in terms of integral kernels.

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{exp}}\nolimits{\left(\int_{0}^{T}\tilde{\psi}(s)\alpha(\psi(s))\,ds+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\tilde{\psi}^{2}(s)\beta^{2}(\psi(s))\,ds\right)}f(\psi(t))

(63)

\displaystyle=\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{N!}{\left(\int_{0}^{T}\tilde{\psi}(s)\alpha(\psi(s))\,ds+\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\tilde{\psi}^{2}(s)\beta^{2}(\psi(s))\,ds\right)}^{N}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{f^{(k)}(u_{0})}{k!}(\psi(t)-u_{0})^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}\sum_{M=0}^{N}\frac{1}{M!(N-M)!}{\left(\int_{0}^{T}\tilde{\psi}(s)\alpha(\psi(s))\,ds\right)}^{M}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt{\left(\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\tilde{\psi}^{2}(s)\beta^{2}(\psi(s))\,ds\right)}^{N-M}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{f^{(k)}(u_{0})}{k!}(\psi(t)-u_{0})^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}\sum_{M=0}^{N}\frac{1}{M!(N-M)!}{\left(\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha^{(l)}(u_{0})}{l!}\int_{0}^{T}\tilde{\psi}(s)(\psi(s)-u_{0})^{l}\,ds\right)}^{M}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\Bigl(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n<k\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{n!k!}\beta^{(n)}(u_{0})\beta^{(k)}(u_{0})\int_{0}^{T}\tilde{\psi}^{2}(s)(\psi(s)-u_{0})^{k+n}\,ds

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(n!)^{2}}(\beta^{(n)}(u_{0}))^{2}\int_{0}^{T}\tilde{\psi}^{2}(s)(\psi(s)-u_{0})^{2n}\,ds\Bigr)^{N-M}

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{f^{(k)}(u_{0})}{k!}(\psi(t)-u_{0})^{k}\,.

In the last identity we exchanged series and integrals by Fubini’s theorem thanks to the absolute convergence of the former under the analyticity assumption on the coefficients and the choice of
\psi
as above. We adopt the short-hand notations

A_{l}:=\int_{0}^{T}\tilde{\psi}(s)(\psi(s)-u_{0})^{l}\,ds\,\qquad B_{j,i}:=\int_{0}^{T}\tilde{\psi}^{2}(s)(\psi(s)-u_{0})^{i+j}\,ds\,.

(64)

Resorting to the multinomial theorem we have

\displaystyle{\left(\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{\alpha^{(l)}(u_{0})}{l!}A_{l}\right)}^{M}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{m_{l}\}_{l\in\mathbb{N}}\\
\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}m_{l}=M\end{subarray}}\frac{M!}{\prod_{l=0}^{\infty}m_{l}!}\prod_{l=1}^{\infty}(\alpha^{(l)}(u_{0}))^{m_{l}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{l!}A_{l}\Bigr)^{m_{l}}\,.

(65)

Analogously, an iterated application of the multinomial theorem on the terms involving
\beta
results in the following more explicit expression

\displaystyle\Bigl(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n<k\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{n!k!}\beta^{(n)}(u_{0})\beta^{(k)}(u_{0})B_{k,n}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(n!)^{2}}(\beta^{(n)}(u_{0}))^{2}B_{n,n}\Bigr)^{N-M}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1},p_{2}\\
p_{1}+p_{2}=N-M\end{subarray}}\frac{(N-M)!}{p_{1}!p_{2}!}\Bigl(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n<k\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{n!k!}\beta^{(n)}(u_{0})\beta^{(k)}(u_{0})B_{k,n}\Bigr)^{p_{1}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(n!)^{2}}(\beta^{(n)}(u_{0}))^{2}B_{n,n}\Bigr)^{p_{2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1},p_{2}\\
p_{1}+p_{2}=N-M\end{subarray}}\frac{(N-M)!}{p_{1}!p_{2}!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{q_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}},\{r_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\\
\sum_{i}q_{i}=p_{1}\\
\sum_{i}r_{i}=p_{2}\end{subarray}}\frac{p_{1}!p_{2}!}{\prod_{i}q_{i}!\prod_{i}r_{i}!}\prod_{i}\Bigl(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n=0\end{subarray}}^{i}\frac{1}{n!i!}\beta^{(n)}(u_{0})\beta^{(i)}(u_{0})B_{n,i}\Bigr)^{q_{i}}

\displaystyle\hskip 227.62204pt\prod_{i}\frac{1}{2^{r_{i}}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{(i!)^{2}}(\beta^{(i)}(u_{0}))^{2}B_{i,i}\Bigr)^{r_{i}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1},p_{2}\\
p_{1}+p_{2}=N-M\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{q_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}},\{r_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\\
\sum_{i}q_{i}=p_{1}\\
\sum_{i}r_{i}=p_{2}\end{subarray}}\frac{(N-M)!\prod_{i}\beta^{(i)}(u_{0})^{2r_{i}}}{\prod_{i}r_{i}!2^{r_{i}}}\prod_{i}\Bigl(\frac{1}{(i!)^{2}}B_{i,i}\Bigr)^{r_{i}}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\prod_{i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{z_{j}\}_{j=0}^{i-1}\\
\sum_{j}z_{j}=q_{i}\end{subarray}}\frac{\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}(\beta^{(j)}(u_{0})\beta^{(i)}(u_{0}))^{z_{j}}}{\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}z_{j}!}\Bigl(\frac{1}{j!i!}B_{j,i}\Bigr)^{z_{j}}\,.

(66)

Plugging (
65
) and (
66
) in (
63
) and rearranging the finite sums, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{N=0}^{\infty}\sum_{M=0}^{N}\frac{1}{M!(N-M)!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{m_{l}\}_{l\in\mathbb{N}}\\
\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}m_{l}=M\end{subarray}}\frac{M!}{\prod_{l=0}^{\infty}m_{l}!}\prod_{l}(\alpha^{(l)}(u_{0}))^{m_{l}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{l!}A_{l}\Bigr)^{m_{l}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p_{1},p_{2}\\
p_{1}+p_{2}=N-M\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{q_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}},\{r_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\\
\sum_{i}q_{i}=p_{1}\\
\sum_{i}r_{i}=p_{2}\end{subarray}}\frac{(N-M)!\prod_{i}\beta^{(i)}(u_{0})^{2r_{i}}}{\prod_{i}r_{i}!2^{r_{i}}}\prod_{i}\frac{1}{2^{r_{i}}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{(i!)^{2}}B_{i,i}\Bigr)^{r_{i}}

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\prod_{i}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{z_{j}\}_{j=0}^{i-1}\\
\sum_{j}z_{j}=q_{i}\end{subarray}}\frac{\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}(\beta^{(j)}(u_{0})\beta^{(i)}(u_{0}))^{z_{j}}}{\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}z_{j}!}\Bigl(\frac{1}{j!i!}B_{j,i}\Bigr)^{z_{j}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{f^{(k)}(u_{0})}{k!}(\psi(t)-u_{0})^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\{m_{l}\}_{l\in\mathbb{N}}\\
\{z^{i}_{j}\}_{(j,i)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}_{<}}\\
\{r_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\prod_{l=0}^{\infty}m_{l}!}\prod_{l}(\alpha^{(l)}(u_{0}))^{m_{l}}\Bigl(\frac{1}{l!}A_{l}\Bigr)^{m_{l}}\frac{\prod_{i}\beta^{(i)}(u_{0})^{2r_{i}}}{\prod_{i}r_{i}!2^{r_{i}}}\prod_{i}\Bigl(\frac{1}{(i!)^{2}}B_{i,i}\Bigr)^{r_{i}}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\prod_{(j,i)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}_{<}}\frac{(\beta^{(j)}(u_{0})\beta^{(i)}(u_{0}))^{z^{i}_{j}}}{z^{i}_{j}!}\Bigl(\frac{1}{j!i!}B_{j,i}\Bigr)^{z^{i}_{j}}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{f^{(k)}(u_{0})}{k!}(\psi(t)-u_{0})^{k}\,.

(67)

As a last step we apply the contraction map
\Gamma_{\Theta}
. Observe that, letting aside for a moment the combinatorial factors, the products of monomial within the series closely resembles the arguments of
\Gamma_{\Theta}
in the definition of the realization map
\Pi
, see (
3.5
). We stress that, since we do not have a convergence result for the expression in (
67
), we define the action of
\Gamma_{\Theta}
on it by bringing the linear operator inside the series. The proof that the ensuing series coincides with the exotic B-series derived in the previous section justifies this ansatz, which is true for every truncated version of the expansion. We can turn the sum over
\{m_{l}\},\{z^{i}_{j}\},\{r_{i}\}
into a sum over Feynman multi-indices via the following identification:

•

\gamma_{\beta}(j,i)=z_{j}^{i}
for all
(j,i)\in\mathbb{N}_{<}^{2}
,

•

\gamma_{\beta}(i,i)=r_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
,

•

\gamma_{\alpha}(l)=m_{l}
for all
l\in\mathbb{N}
,

•

\gamma_{\bullet}(k)=1
and
\gamma_{\bullet}(p)=0
for all
p\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{k\}
,

Hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965588}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965587}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle MSR$}}}}}}[f(u_{t})]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{399347}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{399347}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299972}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}}\frac{\prod_{l}(\alpha^{(l)}(u_{0}))^{\gamma_{\alpha}(l)}(f^{(l)}(u_{0}))^{\gamma_{\bullet}(l)}\prod_{(j,i)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}_{\leq}}(\beta^{(j)}(u_{0})\beta^{(i)}(u_{0}))^{\gamma_{\beta}(j,i)}}{\prod_{l}\gamma_{\alpha}(l)!(l!)^{\gamma_{\alpha}(l)+\gamma_{\bullet}(l)}\prod_{(j,i)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}_{\leq}}\gamma_{\beta}(j,i)!(i!j!)^{\gamma_{\beta}(j,i)}2^{\sum_{i}\gamma_{\beta}(i,i)}k!}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\Gamma_{\Theta}\Bigl(\prod_{l}\Bigl(A_{l}\Bigr)^{\gamma_{\alpha}(l)}\prod_{(j,i)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}_{\leq}}\Bigl(B_{j,i}\Bigr)^{\gamma_{\beta}(j,i)}\prod_{k}(\psi_{t}-u_{0})^{k\gamma_{\bullet}(k)}\Bigr)\Big|_{\begin{subarray}{c}\psi=u_{0}\\
\tilde{\psi}=0\end{subarray}}\,.

Recalling the definitions of
\Upsilon_{u_{0}}^{\alpha,\beta,f},\sigma_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}(z^{\gamma})
and
\Pi_{t}(z^{\gamma})
in (
59
), (
60
) and (
3.5
), respectively we conclude.

Remark 3.9

Since the expansion of MSR expectations reduces to the combinatorial problem of accounting for all possible contractions realized by the map
\Gamma_{\Theta}
, it would be natural to adopt a construction based on MSR Feynman diagrams instead of multi-indices, with the major advantage of adapting it to vector-valued SDEs. Despite this, a formalism based on multi-indices is crucial to handle the combinatorial aspects of the problem, as symmetry factors arise naturally, see the proof of Theorem
3.8
, in stark contrast with
[
BDD25
]
where the use of Feynman diagrams is efficient in the additive and multiplicative case only.

4
Multi-indices exotic B-series

One of the goals of this paper is to draw a comparison between the multi-indices MSR expansion of expectations derived in Section
3.3
and the exotic B-series formulation of the semigroup for the diffusion process obtained in Section
2.4
. The expansion in Corollary
2.29
is close in spirit to the formal series of Theorem
3.8
, the only difference being the set of combinatorial objects used to label their terms. Hence, to conclude our program we must translate the tree-based expansions obtained in the previous sections in the language of multi-indices.

We observe that the exotic coloured trees of Definition
2.8
coincide with the MSR Feynman diagrams introduced in Section
3.1
: They share the same oriented nature, while the exotic decoration codifies the fact that
\beta
appears in the interacting action through its square. Hence it is natural to adopt the Feynman multi-indices of Section
3.3
as the class of combinatorial object that will replace exotic trees. We underline that, since multi-indices encode the crude information of vertex fertility by ignoring the topology of the tree, it is far from obvious how they can codify the exotic decoration, which is intimately linked to the identification of specific pairs of vertices. Yet, the
coloured
nature of Feynman multi-indices encodes both the fertility of
\alpha
-vertices and of pairs of
\beta
-vertices. Guided by the multi-index representation of Feynman diagrams proposed in
[
BH25
]
, we devote this section to a reformulation of the expansion in Corollary
2.29
in terms of multi-indices.

Given an exotic tree
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\mathfrak{e}}
, we must identify the unique Feynman multi-index
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}
such that
\gamma_{\alpha}(n)
coincides with the number of
\alpha
-vertices of
\tau
with
n
incoming edges, while
\gamma_{\beta}(k_{1},k_{2})
matches the number of pairs of
\beta
-vertices linked by a common exotic decoration and with
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
outgoing edges, respectively.

Definition 4.1

We introduce a map
\Psi:\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\alpha,\beta}\to\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}
whose action on
\tau\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\alpha,\beta}
reads

\Psi(\tau):=z_{\bullet,n}\hbox{{\indic 1}${}_{(\mathfrak{n}(\varrho_{\tau})=n)}$}\prod_{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\alpha}(\tau)}z_{\alpha,\mathfrak{n}(v)}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}v_{1},v_{2}\in\mathcal{V}_{\beta}(\tau)\\
\mathfrak{n}(v_{1})\leq\mathfrak{n}(v_{2})\\
\mathfrak{e}(v_{1})=\mathfrak{e}(v_{2})\end{subarray}}z_{\beta,(\mathfrak{n}(v_{1}),\mathfrak{n}(v_{2}))}\,,

where
\mathfrak{n}:\mathcal{V}(\tau)\to\mathbb{N}
counts the fertility of a vertex, see Definition
2.2
.

Remark 4.2

The population condition
3.2
translates at the level of multi-indices the requirement that, to represent Feynman diagrams, the total number of
\tilde{\psi}
-fields and
\psi
-fields must coincide. Since the map
\Psi
of Definition
4.1
practically counts the vertices of the exotic tree, it automatically maps Feynman diagrams into populated multi-indices. One can adapt the procedure devised in
[
BEFH25
, §2]
to prove that the image of
\Psi
coincides with
\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}^{p}
.

We are also interested in a map that does the opposite job, namely that sends a populated Feynman multi-index into all the exotic trees that the multi-index represents. Observe that in general this map will not be injective since, for a fixed set of fertilities, we can build different trees. As an example to bear in mind consider the multi-index
z^{\gamma}=z_{\bullet,1}z_{\alpha,2}z_{\alpha,1}z_{\alpha,2}^{2}
and the trees

It is a simple exercise to check that
\Psi(\tau_{1})=\Psi(\tau_{2})=z^{\gamma}
. Once again we follow the lines of
[
BH25
]
and we introduce a suitable
dual
of
\Psi
, whose form will be justified ultimately by the fact that it provides the result that we need. We refer to
[
ZGM24
]
and
[
BH25
, §3]
for a detailed explanation of why the symmetry factors are there and in which sense the two maps are in duality.

Definition 4.3

The map

\Phi:\mathcal{M}^{p}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}\to\langle\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\alpha,\beta}\rangle
is defined as

\Phi(z^{\gamma}):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\alpha,\beta}\\
\Psi(\tau)=z^{\gamma}\end{subarray}}\frac{\sigma_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}(z^{\gamma})}{\sigma_{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau)}\tau\,,\qquad z^{\gamma}\in\mathcal{M}^{p}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}\,,

(68)

where
\sigma_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}(z^{\gamma})
and
\sigma_{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau)
are the symmetry factors defined in
3.4
and
2.7
, respectively.

In Definition
2.7
we introduced the notion of automorphism of an exotic coloured tree (or Feynman diagram from now on). Since Feynman multi-indices represent pre-Feynman diagrams,
i.e.
collections of vertices with attached free legs that must be joined to form proper diagrams, there is a larger class of transformation that leave the ensuing exotic tree unchanged.

Definition 4.4

(
[
BH25
]
, Def. 4.2)

An isomorphism of the exotic coloured tree
(\tau,\mathfrak{t},{\mathfrak{e}})\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\mathfrak{e}}
is a map that permutes the free legs and vertices of the corresponding pre-Feynman diagram to produce a tree with the same shape as
\tau
and that preserves the endpoints of each free leg, as well as the decorations. They are the composition of two group actions:
g_{\mathcal{V}}
permuting vertices and
g_{\mathcal{E}}
permuting free legs such that

\tau^{\prime}:=g_{\mathcal{V}}\circ g_{\mathcal{E}}\,(\tau)\cong\tau\,,

(69)

We denote by
\text{Iso}(\tau)
the space of isomorphisms of
\tau\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\alpha,\beta}
.

The size of the isomorphism group of
\tau
coincides with
\sigma_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}(\Psi(\tau))
. Indeed, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
(k_{1},k_{2})\in\mathbb{N}^{2}_{\leq}
, the factors
(n!)^{\gamma_{\alpha}(n)}
and
(k_{1}!k_{2}!)^{\gamma_{\beta}(k_{1},k_{2})}
in
\sigma_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}(\Psi(\tau))
correspond to the number of permutations of free legs attached to the vertices, while
\gamma_{\alpha}(n)!
,
\gamma_{\beta}(k_{1},k_{2})!
account for permutations of vertices with the same fertility. Both operations leave the corresponding exotic tree unchanged. A key difference with respect to
[
BH25
]
is the presence of a hidden symmetry responsible for the factor
2^{\sum_{i}\gamma_{\beta}(i,i)}
: if the vertices of
i
i
in the pre-Feynman diagram have the same number of incoming legs, their permutation belongs to
\text{Iso}(\Phi(z^{\gamma}))
and must be accounted for in
\sigma_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}(z^{\gamma})
.

Example 4.5

We propose an example that may help the reader to understand the difference between homomorphisms and isomorphisms of an exotic tree. Let us consider

\tau=\hbox to24.08pt{\vbox to19.92pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 12.04155pt\lower-2.84528pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 3.94 0 C 3.94 2.17 2.17 3.94 0 3.94 C -2.17 3.94 -3.94 2.17 -3.94 0 C -3.94 -2.17 -2.17 -3.94 0 -3.94 C 2.17 -3.94 3.94 -2.17 3.94 0 Z M 0 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

A direct application of Definition
2.7
shows that the only automorphisms of
\tau
are the identity and the exchange of the edged. Hence
\sigma_{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau)=|Aut(\tau)|=2
. To count the number of isomorphisms of
\tau
we break it into the corresponding pre-Feynman diagram:

a
b
\ell_{2}
\ell_{1}
\ell_{4}
\ell_{3}

where we labelled vertices and free legs to keep track of their permutations. Following Definition
4.4
we have the following transformations of the pre-Feynman diagram that preserve the shape of
\tau
:

\lxSVG@closescope {{}}{}{

{{{{{}}{
