\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,.

As a result,
|Iso(\tau)|=4
. By representing
\tau
through the multi-index
z^{\gamma}=z_{\bullet,2}z_{\alpha,0}^{2}
, its symmetry factor as per Definition
3.4
is
\sigma_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}(z^{\gamma})=2!2!=4
, that matches the size of the isomorphism group.

Lemma 4.6

Let
\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\mathfrak{e}}
and let
z^{\gamma}=\Psi(\tau)
. Then, the factor
\frac{\sigma_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{399347}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{399347}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299972}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}(z^{\gamma})}{\sigma_{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau)}
coincides with the number of different ways of linking the free legs of the pre-Feynman diagram represented by
\Psi(\tau)
that yield an exotic tree isomorphic to
\tau
.

Proof.

The proof is identical to the one of
[
BH25
, Prop. 4.3]
, to which we refer for the detains. We sketch it here for completeness.
Given the isomorphism group of
\tau
, its stabilizer
S(\tau)
is the subgroup of transformations that preserve the pairing of free legs, whose cardinality coincides with
\sigma_{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau)
. At the same time the orbits
O(\tau)
of
\tau
are those transformations lying in
Iso(\tau)
that alter the contractions of free legs. In other words,

O(\tau)=\frac{Iso(\tau)}{Aut(\tau)}\,.

The Orbit-Stabilizer Theorem allows to conclude that

\sigma_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}(z^{\gamma})=|Iso(\tau)|=|O(\tau)||Aut(\tau)|=|O(\tau)|\sigma_{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau)\,.

(70)

Hence we conclude by identifying the cardinality of
O(\tau)
with the number of different ways of linking the free legs of the pre-Feynman diagram to obtain
\tau
.

Theorem 4.7

Let
\Pi
and
\Pi^{\mathfrak{e}}
be the realization maps as per Definition
3.5
and Equation
22
, respectively and let us consider the intertwining map
\Phi:\mathcal{M}^{p}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}\to\langle\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\alpha,\beta}\rangle
of Definition (
4.3
). Then, for any
\gamma\in\mathcal{M}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{349810}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle B$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{349810}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle B$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{310230}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle B$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{310229}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle B$}}}}}}
,

\Pi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{t}(\Phi(z^{\gamma}))=\Pi_{t}(z^{\gamma})\,.

(71)

Proof.

Given the action of
\Gamma_{\Theta}
as a map that contracts pairs of free legs
(\tilde{\psi},\psi)
carried by the vertices of the pre-Feynman diagram in all possible ways, we can represent
\Pi_{t}(z^{\gamma})
via a combination of Feynman diagrams obtained by linking in all possible ways the free legs of the pre-Feynman diagram represented by
z^{\gamma}
. This result, which in the case of Gaussian fields coincides with a far-reaching extension of the Wick’s theorem, is known in the literature as Linked cluster theorem, see
[
BK22
, Prop. 3.1]
and references therein. On account of Lemma
4.6
, this remark translates to the following identity

\Pi_{t}(z^{\gamma})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\mathfrak{e}}\\
\Psi(\tau)=z^{\gamma}\end{subarray}}\frac{\sigma_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}(z^{\gamma})}{\sigma_{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau)}\Pi_{t}^{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau)\,,

where the sum is over all non-isomorphic exotic trees and where the factor
\frac{\sigma_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{399347}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{399347}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299972}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}(z^{\gamma})}{\sigma_{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau)}
counts the number of ways of joining free legs to create isomorphic trees.
Then, resorting to the form of
\Phi
and to the linearity of
\Pi_{t}^{\mathfrak{e}}
, we conclude. Note that, unlike in
[
BH25
, Cor. 4.5]
, the proof is simpler since we did not subtract disconnected Feynman diagrams in the definition of
\Pi_{t}
, see Remark
3.6
.

Eventually we are in a position to prove the main result of the paper, namely that the formal path integral expectations computed in the MSR framework coincide with the ones obtained via an Itô-Taylor expansion of expectations with respect to the measure of the diffusion process.

Proposition 4.8

Under the assumptions of Corollary
2.29
, the path integral expectation
\mathbb{E}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965588}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965587}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle MSR$}}}}}}[f(u_{t})]
coincides with the exotic B-series representing
\mathbb{E}^{u_{0}}[f(u_{t})]
, see Corollary
2.29
.

Proof.

First we observe that, for any but fixed
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\mathfrak{e}}
, the associated elementary differential
\Upsilon_{u_{0}}^{\alpha,\beta,f}(\tau)
as per Definition
2.5
is not sensitive to the topology of the tree, but only to the fertility of each vertex. This implies that
\Upsilon_{u_{0}}^{\alpha,\beta,f}(\tau)=\Upsilon_{u_{0}}^{\alpha,\beta,f}(\Psi(\tau))
. Starting from the expression in (
49
) and resorting to Theorem
4.7
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{u_{0}}{\left[f(u_{t})\right]}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\alpha,\beta}}\frac{\Upsilon_{u_{0}}^{\alpha,\beta,f}(\tau)}{\sigma_{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau)}\Pi_{t}(\tau)=\sum_{\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{399347}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{399347}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299972}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\mathcal{T}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\mathfrak{e}}\\
\Psi(\tau)=z^{\gamma}\end{subarray}}\frac{\Upsilon_{u_{0}}^{\alpha,\beta,f}(\tau)}{\sigma_{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau)}\Pi_{t}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{399347}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{399347}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299972}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}}\Upsilon_{u_{0}}^{\alpha,\beta,f}(z^{\gamma})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\alpha,\beta}\\
\Psi(\tau)=z^{\gamma}\end{subarray}}\frac{\Pi_{t}(\tau)}{\sigma_{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{Eq: identity pi phi}}}{{=}}\sum_{\gamma\in\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{399347}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{399347}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299972}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}}\frac{\Upsilon_{u_{0}}^{\alpha,\beta,f}(z^{\gamma})}{\sigma_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}(z^{\gamma})}\Pi_{t}(z^{\gamma})\,.

where the sum is over all of
\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}
as
\Pi
vanishes identically on
\mathcal{M}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}\setminus\mathcal{M}^{p}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{336929}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299974}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle F$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{299973}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle F$}}}}}}
.
Notice that, for the first identity to hold true it is crucial that the map
\Psi
is surjective, see remark
4.2
.

On account of the proper construction of the exotic B-series representing the action of the semigroup, see Theorem
2.24
, Proposition
4.8
guaranteed that the perturbative computation of the MSR expectations as per (
62
) yields the correct result, at each perturbative order. In addition, a comparison with (
2.24
) yields explicit behaviour in time of the truncated series which, for specific instances of the coefficients, may also lead to convergence results for small enough time.

As anticipated in the introduction, the whole derivation of both the exotic Butcher series for the action of the semigroup, together with the result obtained in Proposition
4.8
, provides us with a new perspective on the interpretation of the path integral. To wit, the erroneous interpretation of the MSR path integral as the expectation of the interacting part with respect to a Gaussian measure happens to coincide with the procedure of natural growth at the basis of the construction of (
43
). Indeed, by interpreting legs of the Feynman diagram as the kernels of Heaviside thetas, the contraction of a field
\psi
with the interaction vertices
\tilde{\psi}\alpha(\psi)
and
\tilde{\psi}^{2}\beta^{2}(\psi)
coincides with the grafting of an
\alpha
-vertex and a double
\beta
-vertex, respectively. In addition, the topology of the exotic tree obtained via this contraction procedure gives rise to an iterated integral that, once calculated, coincides with a suitable power in time weighted with the expected combinatorial factor. In conclusion, we should think of the formal series (
62
) as the definition of
\mathbb{E}^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965588}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965587}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle MSR$}}}}}}[f(u_{t})]
.

Appendix A
Reduction of exotic trees and the exotic realization map

In Section
2.5
we completely characterized the combinatorial factor that allows to express the image of the realization map
\Pi_{t}^{\mathfrak{e}}
as a power of
t\in[0,T]
. In doing so, a crucial role was played by the alternative definition of the tree factorial for exotic trees given in Theorem
2.26
. Here we adopt a different point of view based on the form of the integrals codified by closed paths. Before delving into the details, we introduce some notation.

Definition A.1

Let
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\mathfrak{e}}
and let us denote by
\mathsf{C}:{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\mathfrak{e}}\to{\mathcal{F}}_{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\displaystyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{1081250}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\textstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965588}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptstyle MSR$}}}}}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{\resizebox{965587}{262144}{\hbox{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle MSR$}}}}}}
the operator that identifies
\beta
-vertices sharing the same exotic decoration and that forgets the colour decoration
\mathfrak{t}
. We call
closed branch
any closed oriented component
\tau^{\prime}\subseteq\mathsf{C}(\tau)
. Every closed branch has two
closing paths
, defined as the collections of edges connecting the tip of the closed branch to a common vertex.

In order to understand the link between iterated integrals and closed branches formed by pairs of vertices of type
\beta
sharing the same exotic decoration, we take our moves from an explicit computation by considering the tree

\tau=\hbox to33.54pt{\vbox to40.7pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 18.4467pt\lower-2.84528pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,.

(72)

By definition, see (
18
),

\displaystyle\Pi_{t}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,T]^{4}}{\mathcal{G}}(t-l_{1}){\mathcal{G}}(l_{1}-l_{2}){\mathcal{G}}(t-r_{1}){\mathcal{G}}(r_{1}-r_{2})\delta(r_{2}-l_{2})\,dr_{1}dr_{2}dl_{1}dl_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,T]^{3}}{\mathcal{G}}(t-l_{1}){\mathcal{G}}(l_{1}-l_{2}){\mathcal{G}}(t-r_{1}){\mathcal{G}}(r_{1}-l_{2})\,dr_{1}dl_{1}dl_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,T]^{3}}\hbox{{\indic 1}${}_{(l_{2}\leq r_{1}\leq t)}$}\hbox{{\indic 1}${}_{(l_{2}\leq l_{1}\leq t)}$}\,dr_{1}dl_{1}dl_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,T]^{3}}\hbox{{\indic 1}${}_{(l_{2}\leq r_{1}\leq l_{1}\leq t)}$}\,dr_{1}dl_{1}dl_{2}+\int_{[0,T]^{3}}\hbox{{\indic 1}${}_{(l_{2}\leq l_{1}\leq r_{1}\leq t)}$}\,dr_{1}dl_{1}dl_{2}=2\cdot\frac{t^{3}}{3!}\,.

We can immediately generalize the information carried by this simple example by observing that the integral represented by a closed branch coincides with a combination of iterated integrals over all possible permutations of the integration variables corresponding to the internal vertices of the closed branch, that maintain their internal order within the closing paths. This specific type of permutation of two sets, referred to as
shuffle product
, is crucial in the algebraic formulation of Chen’s relation for products of iterated integrals labelled by words
[
FH20
]
. Observe that the integral codified by a closed branch is almost like a product of iterated integrals, with the exotic decoration acting like an additional constraint. Therefore
\Pi_{t}(\tau)
coincides with
\binom{\mathfrak{l}(\tau_{1})+\mathfrak{l}(\tau_{2})-2}{\mathfrak{l}(\tau_{1})-1}\frac{t^{\mathfrak{l}(\tau)-1}}{(\mathfrak{l}(\tau)-1)!}
, where
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
are the closing paths of
\tau
. The binomial factor coincides with the number of ways of shuffling the edges of the closing paths, i.e. of sorting them by preserving the relative orders.

This observation suggests that the integral represented by a closed branch reduces to an iterated integral represented by
B_{+}^{\mathfrak{l}(\tau)-1}(\bullet)
, the latter denoting
\mathfrak{l}(\tau)-1
applications of the operation that grafts on an undecorated root. Note that, if the closed path does not have subtrees attached to it, all possible outcomes are identical. However, this is not true in the opposite case, where the choice of where to insert a specific vertex leads to a different tree lying in
{\mathcal{T}}
. For this reason we introduce the following
reduction of exotic trees
.

Definition A.2

We define a reduction operator
\mathsf{R}:{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\mathfrak{e}}\to\langle{\mathcal{T}}\rangle
defined via the following algorithm:

1.

for any
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\mathfrak{e}}
, we turn it into an MSR Feynman diagram via the operator
\mathsf{C}
of Definition
A.1

2.

starting from the root of
\mathsf{C}(\tau)
and following the direction of the tree backwards, we search for the first closed branch. Then, picking one of the closing paths, we insert each of its vertices inside the edges composing the other closing path in all possible ways by respecting the original order.

3.

we repeat this procedure for all closed branches.

We denote by
\mathcal{R}(\tau)
the family of trees obtained from
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\mathfrak{e}}
via the reduction operation.

Remark A.3

The number of trees obtained from the shuffle product between the vertices of the two closing paths of a closed branch with
n
and
m
internal edges, respectively coincides with
\binom{n+m-2}{n-1}
.

where the factor
2
derives from the fact that inserting the only internal vertex of one branch above or below the one of the other does not change the structure of the ensuing tree. It is useful to go through an example in which such distinction is crucial:

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,.

From what we observed above, given
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\mathfrak{e}}
and denoting by
\mathcal{R}(\tau)\subset{\mathcal{T}}
the space of trees obtained via the reduction operator
\mathsf{R}
, it follows that

\Pi_{t}^{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau)=\sum_{\bar{\tau}\in\mathcal{R}(\tau)}\Pi_{t}(\bar{\tau})\,,

(73)

where
\Pi_{t}(\bar{\tau})
is the branched integral represented by the non-decorated tree
\bar{\tau}
. Interestingly enough, the explicit expression of the realization map on
{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha,\beta}^{\mathfrak{e}}
derived in Proposition
2.27
implies the following result, which is of independent interest.

Lemma A.5

Let
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathfrak{e}}_{\alpha,\beta}
and let
\mathcal{R}(\tau)
the family of trees obtained from
\tau
via the reduction operator
\mathsf{R}
, see Definition
A.2
. Then

\frac{|\tau|!}{\tau!}=\sum_{\bar{\tau}\in\mathcal{R}(\tau)}\frac{|\bar{\tau}|!}{\bar{\tau}!}\,.

(74)

Proof.

The evaluation maps in the right-hand side of (
73
) fall under the scope of (
46
) by regarding
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}
as a tree lying in
{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha}\subset{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha,\beta}
by identifying all the vertices aside from the root with
\alpha
-decorated ones. Then

\Pi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{t}(\tau)=\sum_{\bar{\tau}\in\mathcal{R}(\tau)}\frac{|\bar{\tau}|!}{\bar{\tau}!}\frac{t^{\mathfrak{l}(\bar{\tau})}}{\mathfrak{l}(\bar{\tau})!}\,.

At the same time, (
46
) guarantees that
\Pi^{\mathfrak{e}}_{t}(\tau)=\frac{|\tau|!}{\tau!}\frac{t^{\mathfrak{l}(\tau)}}{\mathfrak{l}(\tau)!}
. Recalling that
\mathsf{R}
preserves the number of edges we conclude.

Appendix B
Examples

To show the strength of the exotic B-series representation of expectations of the solution to an Itô diffusion, we analyse how our construction specializes to situations where the statistics of the solution is known. In the following examples the asymptotic series (
43
) converges uniformly in time.
Until now, by working with generic coefficients
\alpha
and
\beta
in (
1
), no constraints on the topology of the exotic trees used to label the B-series were imposed. In practice, the form of the coefficients forces us to adopt a
rule
,
i.e.
, a restriction on the admissible fertility of the vertices that compose the tree.

Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process

Let us consider the well known Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process, defined as the solution to the initial value problem on
\mathbb{R}_{+}

\begin{cases}du_{t}=-au_{t}dt+\sigma dW_{t}\,,\qquad\\
u(0)=u_{0}\end{cases}\,,

(75)

where
a,\sigma>0
are non-negative constants. To make contact with the notation of the previous sections we set
\alpha(u_{t}):=-au_{t}
and
\beta(u_{t}):=\sigma
. Here we can better understand the notion of rule: given the construction of the Itô-Taylor expansion of Section
2.1
in terms of differential operators and their interpretation as grafting operations, it follows that vertices of type
\alpha
can have either zero or one incoming edges, while
\beta
-vertices must have fertility zero. An additional rule is set by the choice of the smooth function
f
. Being interested in the expectation of the process, let us consider first
f(u):=u
. At the level of trees, this translates into asking for the root to have at most one incoming edge as well. As a result, the only trees allowed are linear trees obtained by concatenation of
\alpha
-vertices. For any
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\alpha}
of this form it immediately follows that

\frac{|\tau|!}{\sigma_{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau)\,\tau!}=1\,,\qquad\Upsilon_{u_{0}}^{\alpha,\beta,f}(\tau)=u_{0}(-a)^{|\tau|-1}\,.

Observing that simple linear trees can be identified with their number of vertices,
the expression in (
43
) reduces to

\mathbb{E}^{u_{0}}[u_{t}]=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(-a)^{n-1}u_{0}\frac{t^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}=u_{0}e^{-at}\,.

For what concerns the variance of
u_{t}
the rule changes, since in this case we consider
f(u_{t})=u_{t}^{2}
, so that the root can have up to
2
incoming edges. This times the only trees that we can builds according to the rule have the form

.
.
.

.
.
.
.
.
.

.
.
.
.
.
.
1
1

where the dots denote that we can concatenate an arbitrary number of
\alpha
-vertices. Note that, since
\beta
-vertices can only close the concatenation, we can practically forget about them when computing the combinatorial part of the series. As for the calculation of the expectation of
u_{t}
, the sum over all linear trees is rather simple. Yet one must recall that this time the elementary differential is different in that the derivative of
u^{2}
produces a factor
2
. Then the contribution from the root and all the concatenations of
\alpha
-vertices yields

u_{0}^{2}+2u_{0}^{2}(e^{-at}-1)\,,

Now consider all trees of the second kind above. This time, to account for the symmetry factor, we must distinguish the case where the number of vertices on both sides of the tree coincide or not. Denoting by
{\mathcal{T}}^{2}
the second family of trees,

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}^{2}}\frac{|\tau|!}{\sigma_{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau)\tau!}

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{u_{0}}^{\alpha,\beta,f}(\tau)\frac{t^{\mathfrak{l}(\tau)}}{\mathfrak{l}(\tau)!}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\sum_{m=1}^{n-1}\frac{(n+m)!}{n!m!}2u_{0}^{2}(-a)^{n+m}\frac{t^{n+m}}{(n+m)!}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{2n!}{2(n!)^{2}}2u_{0}^{2}(-a)^{2n}\frac{t^{2n}}{2n!}

\displaystyle=2u_{0}^{2}\sum_{n=2}^{\infty}\sum_{m=1}^{n-1}\frac{(-at)^{n+m}}{n!m!}+u_{0}^{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-at)^{2n}}{(n!)^{2}}

By completing the sums with
n=0,1
and
m=0
and adding the contribution from linear trees computed above, one obtains

\displaystyle u_{0}^{2}+2u_{0}^{2}(e^{-at}-1)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}^{2}}\frac{|\tau|!}{\sigma_{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau)\tau!}\Upsilon_{u_{0}}^{\alpha,\beta,f}(\tau)\frac{t^{\mathfrak{l}(\tau)}}{\mathfrak{l}(\tau)!}

\displaystyle=2u_{0}^{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}\frac{(-at)^{n+m}}{n!m!}+u_{0}^{2}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-at)^{2n}}{(n!)^{2}}=u_{0}^{2}e^{-2at}\,.

where the last identity follows by a clever manipulation of the Taylor expansion of
u_{0}^{2}e^{-2at}
. This contribution coincides with the square of the expectation value, which is subtracted when computing the variance.
Moving to trees belonging to the third family, that we denote by
{\mathcal{T}}^{3}
, an analogous reasoning applies. Once again we must break down the sum into symmetric and non-symmetric trees, to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}^{3}}\frac{|\tau|!}{\sigma_{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau)\tau!}

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{u_{0}}^{\alpha,\beta,f}(\tau)\frac{t^{\mathfrak{l}(\tau)}}{\mathfrak{l}(\tau)!}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}\frac{(n+m)!}{n!m!}2\sigma^{2}(-a)^{n+m}\frac{t^{n+m-1}}{(n+m-1)!}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(2n)!}{2n!^{2}}2\sigma^{2}(-a)^{2n}\frac{t^{2n+1}}{(2n+1)!}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\sigma^{2}}{a}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}\frac{(-at)^{n+m+1}}{(n+m+1)n!m!}-\frac{\sigma^{2}}{a}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-at)^{2n+1}}{(2n+1)n!^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sigma}{a}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-at)^{n+m+1}}{(n+m+1)n!m!}\,.

On the one hand, by the change of variable
k=n+m
the last expression can be rewritten as

\displaystyle-\frac{\sigma}{a}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-at)^{n+m+1}}{(n+m+1)n!m!}=-\frac{\sigma}{a}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n=0}^{k}\frac{(-at)^{k+1}}{n!(k-n)!(k+1)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\sigma}{a}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-at)^{k+1}2^{k}}{(k+1)k!}=\sigma\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-2a)^{k}}{k!}\int_{0}^{t}x^{k}\,dx=\frac{\sigma}{2a}(1-e^{-2\sigma t})\,,

Putting all contributions together we obtain the exact expression for the variance of the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process

\mathbb{E}^{u_{0}}[u^{2}_{t}]-(\mathbb{E}^{u_{0}}[u_{t}])^{2}=\frac{\sigma}{2a}(1-e^{-2\sigma t})\,.

Geometric Brownian motion

Anther relevant example in which the measure of the solution can be determined explicitely is the
geometric Brownian motion

\begin{cases}du_{t}=au_{t}dt+\sigma u_{t}dW_{t}\,,\qquad\\
u(0)=u_{0}\end{cases}\,,

(76)

where
a,\sigma\geq 0
are again non-negative constants. The solution can be readily derived via a logarithmic change of variable and Itô’s formula. Instead of mimicking the line of reasoning adopted for the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process, we focus on a natural feature of problems with a linear drift. When writing the solution in its weak form as a stochastic convolution, nothing would prevent us from including the linear part into the differential operator. If we do so, the weak solution is obtained by convolving with the Green’s function of the modified operator, see
[
BDD25
, §2.1]
where this point of view is justified from the standpoint of Feynman diagrams via the introduction of effective edges. We can give a motivation of this choice by recalling that, as proved in Theorem
2.24
, the contributions to the Itô-Taylor series are in one-to-one correspondence with exotic coloured trees that abide by the rule imposed by the explicit form of
\alpha
and
\beta
. For a linear drift, a vertex
\alpha
cannot give rise to a bifurcation since it carries a single
\psi
-leg. Let us focus on the simpler tree with a single vertex
\alpha
, that represents a contribution of the form
\int_{0}^{t}\alpha(u_{0})f^{(1)}(u_{0})\,ds
. When expanding at higher orders the only effect of the linear part is to add internal vertices
\alpha
to form linear trees as the ones in the previous example. In other words we can group the contributions

up to an arbitrary number of
\alpha
-vertices. The analytic contribution to this effective edge reads

au_{0}f^{(1)}(u_{0})\int_{0}^{t}{\left(1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a^{n}\int_{[0,s]^{n}}ds_{n}\ldots ds_{1}\right)}\,ds=au_{0}f^{(1)}(u_{0})\int_{0}^{t}e^{as}\,ds\,.

Hence, the net effect of adding up all trees that differ by the number of
\alpha
-vertices between two fixed consecutive vertices is to substitute the edge with an effective one that encodes integration against the exponential kernel
e^{as}
. This procedure can be carried over for all edges, hence leaving as with trees involving only effective edges and
\beta
-vertices. An important difference is that the elementary differential becomes time-dependent: each derivative of the coefficient represented by a vertex must be evaluated at
u_{0}e^{as}
, where
s
is the integration variable that it carries. We can justify this choice by observing that, after this change of paradigm, the expectation value of
u
receives a single contribution, the one from the root. Hence we should incorporate the elementary differential inside the realization map, in such a way that
\tilde{\Pi}_{t}(\bullet):=u_{0}e^{at}
. The definition of
\tilde{\Pi}

on trees
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\beta}^{\mathfrak{e}}
with
|\tau|>1

differs from the one in (
22
) by the presence of derivatives of
\beta
evaluated at
u_{0}e^{as}
for the integrated internal variable
s
.
This simplification, that we could have exploited also in the previous example, becomes crucial here: this time the rule enforced by (
76
) allows for bifurcations stemming from
\beta
-vertices. Hence, without this reduction, we could have trees with an arbitrary number of closed paths and
\alpha
-vertices, making the explicit resummation of the B-series unpractical.
For what concerns
f(u_{t})=u_{t}^{2}
, the only family of exotic trees that we can construct with the sole
\beta
-vertices have the form

1
1
.
.
.
.
.
.
n
n

where the dots stand for an arbitrary number of pairs
i
i
. For any
\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}_{\beta}
of this form such that
|\tau|=n
we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sigma_{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau)}\tilde{\Pi}_{t}^{\mathfrak{e}}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{[0,t]^{n-1}}2\sigma^{2(n-1)}u_{0}^{2}e^{2as_{n}}e^{2a(t-s_{1})}\ldots e^{2a(s_{n-1}-s_{n})}\,ds_{n}\ldots ds_{1}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2(n-1)}u_{0}^{2}e^{2at}\frac{t^{n-1}}{(n-1)!}\,,

where
2\sigma^{2(n-1)}u_{0}^{2}e^{2as_{n}}
is the new, time-dependent contribution to
\tilde{\Pi}
. As a result

\mathbb{E}^{u_{0}}[u_{t}^{2}]-(\mathbb{E}^{u_{0}}[u_{t}])^{2}=u_{0}^{2}e^{2at}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(\sigma^{2}t)}{n!}-u_{0}^{2}e^{2at}=e^{2at}(e^{\sigma^{2}t}-1)\,,

in accordance with the result that one would obtain via Itô’s lemma.

Remark B.1

In Section
2.4.1
we observed that the asymptotic nature of the power series expansion of the semigroup is ascribed to the growth rate of the number of trees with a fixed number of vertices as the latter increases. The reason why the series converges when computing the expectation and second moment of the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process and the of geometric Brownian motion resides in the specific rule imposed by the linear nature of the coefficients. Indeed, when the maximum number of edges stemming from each vertex is fixed, as it is the case for polynomial drift and diffusivity, the number of trees having a fixed number of vertices is tamed.

Appendix C
Distribution-valued functionals and contraction maps

To establish the notation adopted in this section, the universal tensor algebras over
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R})
and
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
are defined as

\mathsf{T}(\mathbb{R}):=\mathbb{R}\oplus\bigoplus_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R})^{\otimes n}\,,\hskip 42.67912pt\mathsf{T}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}):=\mathbb{R}\oplus\bigoplus_{n=1}^{\infty}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})^{\otimes n}\,.

(77)

In this section we introduce the key notions that allow us to reformulate the MSR path integral in terms of suitable functionals and contraction maps. The interested reader can consult
[
DDRZ22
,
BDD25
]
for a more detailed account of these notions.

Definition C.1

A distribution-valued functional is a map

F:C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\times C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathsf{T}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})\,.

We denote by
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})
the space of distribution-valued functionals and we endow it with the
multilocal product

(F\cdot H)(\psi,\tilde{\psi}):=F(\psi,\tilde{\psi})\otimes H(\psi,\tilde{\psi})\,,\qquad F,H\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime}),\psi,\tilde{\psi}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\,,

where
\otimes
denotes the tensor product of distributions.

In the following, given a functional
F\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})
and arbitrary field configurations
\psi,\tilde{\psi}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, we denote by
F^{(1,0)}(\psi,\tilde{\psi}),F^{(0,1)}(\psi,\tilde{\psi}):C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
its Frechet derivatives
3
3

3

Henceforth we shall assume that all distribution-valued functionals of our interest admit Frechet derivatives of all orders. For a detailed discussion on the topologies on space of functionals considered in quantum field theory we refer to
[
BDLGR18
]

F^{(1,0)}(\psi,\tilde{\psi},\varphi):=\frac{d}{ds}F(\psi+s\varphi,\tilde{\psi})\Big|_{s=0}\,,\qquad F^{(0,1)}(\psi,\tilde{\psi},\varphi):=\frac{d}{ds}F(\psi,\tilde{\psi}+s\varphi)\Big|_{s=0}\,.

Higher order derivatives
F^{(n,m)}
for
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
are defined accordingly. For what concerns the adoption of this framework to the problem at hand, it suffices to consider the following sub-class of distribution-valued functionals.

Definition C.2

Let
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})
be the space of distribution-valued functionals as per Definition
C.1
. We denote by
\mathcal{F}_{mloc}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})
the space of
multi-local distribution-valued functionals

F
of the form

\displaystyle\langle F(\psi,\tilde{\psi}),f\rangle=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}f^{n}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\,p^{n}(\psi(t_{1}),\tilde{\psi}(t_{1}),\ldots,\psi(t_{n}),\tilde{\psi}(t_{n}))\,dt_{1}\ldots dt_{n}\,,

for all
f\in\mathsf{T}(\mathbb{R})
,
\psi,\tilde{\psi}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Here
p^{n}
are fixed polynomials, while
f^{n}
denotes the n-th component of
f
in
\mathsf{T}(\mathbb{R})
. In addition, we denote by
\mathcal{F}_{loc}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})\subset\mathcal{F}_{mloc}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})
the subspace of functionals taking values in
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R})
.

A special role is played by the
field functionals

\langle\Psi(\psi,\tilde{\psi}),f\rangle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\psi(t)f(t)\,dt\,,\qquad\langle\tilde{\Psi}(\psi,\tilde{\psi}),f\rangle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\tilde{\psi}(t)f(t)\,dt\,.

(78)

and by the
constant functional

\langle\mathbf{1}(\psi,\tilde{\psi}),f\rangle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(t)\,dt\,.

(79)

These functionals have vanishing components in
\mathsf{T}^{\prime}\setminus{\mathcal{D}}(\mathbb{R})
.
A direct computation shows that
\Psi,\tilde{\Psi}\in\mathcal{F}_{loc}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})
. For any
h\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R})
we define the composition with the field functionals as

\langle h(\Psi,\tilde{\Psi})(\psi,\tilde{\psi}),f\rangle:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(\psi(s),\tilde{\psi}(s))f(s)\,ds\,.

Notation C.3

For notational ease, since we will work extensively with multilocal, polynomial distribution-valued functionals
F\in\mathcal{F}_{mloc}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})
, we shall adopt a notation based on their integral kernel counterpart. Namely, we represent their
n
-th component as
F^{(n)}[\psi,\widetilde{\psi}](t_{1},\ldots t_{n})
,
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\psi,\tilde{\psi}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
, formally defined via the relation

\langle F^{(n)}[\psi,\widetilde{\psi}],f\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n}}F^{(n)}[\psi,\widetilde{\psi}](t_{1},\ldots t_{n})f(t_{1}\ldots t_{n})\,dt_{1}\ldots dt_{n},\hskip 28.45274ptf\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}^{n}).

(80)

Pursuing the analogy with Gaussian integration and Wick’s theorem, the computation of expectation values with respect to the free dynamics should boils down to maximally
contracting smooth fields
\psi
with the auxiliary field
\tilde{\psi}
in all possible ways. This operation can be formalized via a deformation of the algebraic structure of the spaces of distribution-valued functionals.

Definition C.4

We define the
contraction map

\Gamma_{\Theta}:\mathcal{F}_{mloc}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})\to\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})
as the operator exponential

\Gamma_{\Theta}:=\mathop{\mathrm{exp}}\nolimits(D_{\Theta})\,,

where
D_{\Theta}
is a differential operator in the Frechet topology of
\mathcal{F}^{mloc}
whose action reads

\langle D_{\Theta}F(\psi,\tilde{\psi}),f\rangle:=\langle F^{(1,1)}(\psi,\tilde{\psi}),f\otimes\Theta\rangle\,,\hskip 42.67912ptf\in\mathsf{T}(\mathbb{R}),\psi,\tilde{\psi}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\,,

(81)

or, in the kernel-based notation,

D_{\Theta}(F)(\psi,\tilde{\psi}):=\int_{[0,T]^{2}}\Theta(t-t^{\prime})F^{(1,1)}(\psi,\tilde{\psi})(t,t^{\prime})\,dtdt^{\prime}\,.

Note that the expression in (
81
) is a convergent integral, since by Definition
C.2
it holds that
supp\langle F^{(1,1)}(\psi,\tilde{\psi}),f\rangle\subset supp(f)
.

The following standard result justifies out statement that
\Gamma_{\Theta}
is a deformation of the algebraic structure induces by the multilocal product. We refer the reader to
[
DDR20
,
DDRZ22
]
for the proof, that relies on the exponential form of the deformation.

Proposition C.5

Let us introduce the space

\mathcal{F}_{\Theta}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime}):=\{\Gamma_{\Theta}(F)\,|\;F\in\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})\}\,.

Then,
\mathcal{F}_{\Theta}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})
forms an associative algebra if endowed with the product
\star_{\Theta}:\mathcal{F}_{\Theta}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})\times\mathcal{F}_{\Theta}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})\to\mathcal{F}_{\Theta}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})
implicitely defined via the identity

F\star_{\Theta}H:=\Gamma_{\Theta}{\left[\Gamma_{\Theta}^{-1}(F)\cdot\Gamma_{\Theta}^{-1}(H)\right]}\,,\hskip 42.67912pt\forall F,H\in\mathcal{F}_{\Theta}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})\,.

Remark C.6

If
F\in\mathcal{F}_{mloc}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})
, Definition
C.2
entails that
F^{(1,1)}(\psi,\tilde{\psi})
is a distribution supported on the diagonal. Thus, the action of
D_{\Theta}
on
\mathcal{F}_{mloc}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})
is a priori hindered by the presence of the ill-defined value of
\Theta
at the origin. Formally, this can be pictured as having to cope with the product of distributions
\theta(t-t^{\prime})\delta(t-t^{\prime})
, which we shall denote with a slight abuse of notation as
\Theta(0)
. The latter is evidently ill-defined since different regularizations
\Theta_{\varepsilon}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
of
\Theta
obtained via the convolution with a mollifier
\varrho_{\varepsilon}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R})
lead to different limiting values
\theta(0):=\mathop{\mathrm{lim}}_{\varepsilon\to 0}\theta_{\varepsilon}\delta
.
This arbitrariness is rather natural and reflects an analogous freedom in adopting a specific finite-dimensional approximation of the SDE in the heuristic derivation of the MSR path integral, see
e.g.

[
LL07
]
. In Definition
C.4
we are implicitly considering an arbitrary but fixed choice of
\Theta(0)
. Yet, different extensions of the Heaviside theta lead to different contraction maps. We observe that any particular choice of
\Theta(0)
does not constitute a loss of generality, we recall that Proposition
C.5
guarantees that different choices lead to isomorphic algebras. More specifically, for any but fixes
\Theta,\bar{\Theta}
such that
\Theta(t)=\bar{\Theta}(t)
for all
t\neq 0
, the isomorphism between
(\mathcal{F}_{\Theta}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime}),\star_{\Theta})
and
(\mathcal{F}_{\bar{\Theta}}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime}),\star_{\bar{\theta}})
is realized by the map
\Gamma_{\bar{\Theta}-\Theta}
in the sense that, for any
F_{1},F_{2}\in\mathcal{F}_{\Theta}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\bar{\Theta}-\Theta}{\left[\Gamma_{\bar{\Theta}-\Theta}^{-1}(F_{1})\star_{\Theta}\Gamma_{\bar{\Theta}-\Theta}^{-1}(F_{2})\right]}=F_{1}\star_{\bar{\Theta}}F_{2}\,.

This sheds some light on an interesting interpretation of the arbitrariness in defining a stochastic integration theory as an example of renormalization involving finite counterterms. We postpone further investigations on the connection between stochastic integral prescriptions and renormalization to a future work.

We introduce a nonlocal version of the contraction map of Definition (
C.4
)

\Gamma_{\Theta}^{mloc}:\mathcal{F}_{mloc}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})\,\hat{\otimes}\,\mathcal{F}_{mloc}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{mloc}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})\,,

whose action on
F_{1},F_{2}\in\mathcal{F}_{mloc}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})
reads

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\Theta}^{mloc}(F_{1}\hat{\otimes}F_{2}):=m\circ e^{D^{mloc}_{\Theta}}(F_{1}\hat{\otimes}F_{2}),

(82)

\displaystyle D^{mloc}_{\Theta}(F_{1}\hat{\otimes}F_{2})((\psi,\tilde{\psi})\otimes(\psi^{\prime},\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}))

(83)

\displaystyle\hskip 51.21504pt:=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Theta(t,t^{\prime}){\left(F_{1}^{(1,0)}(t)\hat{\otimes}F_{2}^{(0,1)}(t^{\prime})+F_{1}^{(0,1)}(t)\,\hat{\otimes}\,F_{2}^{(1,0)}(t^{\prime})\right)}((\psi,\tilde{\psi})\otimes(\psi^{\prime},\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}))\,dtdt^{\prime},

(84)

for
\psi,\tilde{\psi},\psi^{\prime},\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
. Here
\hat{\otimes}
denotes the tensor product of functionals

\langle(F_{1}\hat{\otimes}F_{2})((\psi,\tilde{\psi})\otimes(\psi^{\prime},\tilde{\psi}^{\prime})),f\otimes g\rangle:=\langle F_{1}(\psi,\tilde{\psi}),f\rangle\langle F_{2}(\psi^{\prime},\tilde{\psi}^{\prime}),g\rangle\,,

while

m:\mathcal{F}_{mloc}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})\hat{\otimes}\mathcal{F}_{mloc}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{mloc}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T}^{\prime})
is the pull-back on the diagonal of
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})\times C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
.
The contraction maps
\Gamma_{\Theta}
and
\Gamma_{\Theta}^{mloc}
are linked by the identity

\Gamma_{\Theta}(F_{1}\cdot F_{2})=\Gamma_{\Theta}^{mloc}{\left(\Gamma_{\Theta}(F_{1})\hat{\otimes}\Gamma_{\Theta}(F_{2})\right)},

(85)

for all
F_{1},F_{2}\in\mathcal{F}_{loc}(\mathbb{R},\mathsf{T})
.
At the practical level, the relation in (
85
) states that we can separate
local contractions
of fields
\psi
and
\widetilde{\psi}
belonging to the same local distribution-valued functional
F_{i}
from
nonlocal
contractions between
F_{1}
and
F_{2}
.

\endappendix
